```text
1	\section{Method: The SICA Pipeline}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	This section describes Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), the pipeline we implement and evaluate as a representative instantiation of self-extracted symbolic consensus. SICA extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from multiple reasoning traces produced by a single LLM, formulates a weighted partial MAX-SAT problem over these constraints, and selects the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset. We compare SICA against Self-Consistency \cite{wang2023sc}, which selects the majority answer across the same set of traces.
5	
6	\subsection{Overview}
7	\label{sec:method-overview}
8	
9	Given a question $q$, SICA proceeds in four stages. First, an LLM generates $K$ independent reasoning traces $\{t_1, \ldots, t_K\}$ by sampling at temperature $T$; each trace $t_i$ yields a candidate answer $a_i$. Second, the same LLM is prompted to extract a set of structured FOL constraints $C_i$ from each trace $t_i$. Third, constraints are deduplicated across traces by syntactic equivalence matching, and each unique constraint receives a weight equal to the number of traces that produced it. Fourth, the deduplicated constraint pool is formulated as a weighted partial MAX-SAT instance and solved by Z3 \cite{demoura2008z3}. Each candidate answer is then scored by the total weight of satisfied constraints that support it, and the highest-scoring answer is selected.
10	
11	Self-Consistency (SC) uses only the first stage: it selects $a^* = \arg\max_{a} \sum_{i=1}^{K} \mathbf{1}[a_i = a]$, the answer produced by the most traces. SICA's additional signal comes from the constraint satisfaction structure. Whether this additional signal provides discriminative power beyond vote counting is the central empirical question of this paper.
12	
13	\subsection{Constraint Extraction}
14	\label{sec:constraint-extraction}
15	
16	For each reasoning trace $t_i$, the same LLM that generated $t_i$ is prompted to extract a set of structured FOL constraints. While we prompt the model to produce FOL-style formalizations, the extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers, reflecting the capabilities of instruction-scale models. The extraction prompt requests JSON-formatted output containing the constraint type (equation, inequality, or predicate), a human-readable expression, and a Z3-compatible formula string. Each constraint $c$ is associated with its source trace $t_i$ and the answer $a_i$ that trace produced. A representative extraction output is:
17	\begin{small}
18	\begin{verbatim}
19	{"type": "predicate",
20	 "nl": "Charlie is kind",
21	 "z3": "kind_charlie == True"}
22	\end{verbatim}
23	\end{small}
24	\noindent The full extraction prompt template is provided in Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}.
25	
26	We define the \emph{extraction rate} as the fraction of traces from which at least one parseable constraint is successfully extracted. Traces that yield no valid constraints are excluded from the MAX-SAT stage but still contribute their answer to the candidate pool.
27	
28	Deduplication proceeds by syntactic equivalence matching across all extracted constraints. For each unique constraint $c_m$, its weight $w_m$ equals the number of distinct traces that produced a syntactically equivalent constraint. This weighting reflects the intuition that constraints appearing in many independent reasoning paths are more likely to encode genuine problem structure.
29	
30	\subsection{Weighted Partial MAX-SAT Formulation}
31	\label{sec:maxsat}
32	
33	Let $\mathcal{A}(q) = \{a : a \in \{a_1, \ldots, a_K\}\}$ denote the set of distinct candidate answers for question $q$, and let $\mathcal{C} = \{c_1, \ldots, c_M\}$ denote the deduplicated constraint pool with associated weights $\{w_1, \ldots, w_M\}$.
34	
35	\paragraph{MAX-SAT instance.} We formulate a Weighted Partial MAX-SAT (WPMS) problem where each unique constraint $c_m$ serves as a soft clause with weight $w_m$. The objective is to find the largest-weight subset $\mathcal{C}^* \subseteq \mathcal{C}$ such that all constraints in $\mathcal{C}^*$ are jointly satisfiable:
36	\begin{equation}
37	\label{eq:maxsat}
38	\mathcal{C}^* = \arg\max_{\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{C}} \sum_{c \in \mathcal{S}} w_c \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \mathcal{S} \text{ is satisfiable.}
39	\end{equation}
40	Z3's optimization engine solves this instance with a per-problem timeout of 10 seconds.
41	
42	\paragraph{Answer scoring.} Each candidate answer $a \in \mathcal{A}(q)$ is scored by the total weight of constraints in $\mathcal{C}^*$ that support it:
43	\begin{equation}
44	\label{eq:score}
45	\text{score}(a) = \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^*} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a),
46	\end{equation}
47	where $\sigma(c, a) \in \{0, 1\}$ is determined by Z3: $\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if constraint $c$ is satisfiable under the assignment implied by answer $a$. Each constraint is evaluated against every candidate answer, so a constraint extracted from one trace can in principle support or exclude answers from other traces. The selected answer is $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \text{score}(a)$.
48	
49	\paragraph{Cross-answer constraint evaluation.}
50	A constraint $c_m$ extracted from trace $t_i$ (which produced answer $a_i$) must in principle be evaluated against \emph{all} candidate answers, not only $a_i$. In practice, however, self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answer. Because $c_m$ is derived from the reasoning steps that led to $a_i$, the semantic content of $c_m$ typically entails $a_i$. As a result, $\sigma(c_m, a_i) \approx 1$ holds for the source answer, while $\sigma(c_m, a_j)$ for $a_j \neq a_i$ depends on whether $c_m$ happens to also be consistent with $a_j$.
51	
52	This coupling has a critical consequence. When constraints lack discriminative power (i.e., $\sigma(c, a)$ is approximately constant across candidates, or constraints from different traces supporting different answers cancel out), the score function degenerates:
53	\begin{equation}
54	\label{eq:degenerate}
55	\text{score}(a) \approx \sum_{\substack{c \in \mathcal{C}^* \\ \text{source}(c) \to a}} w_c,
56	\end{equation}
57	which reduces to a weighted count of traces supporting $a$. If constraint weights are roughly uniform, this is equivalent to majority voting. In practice, because self-extracted constraints predominantly encode domain facts rather than answer-discriminative predicates, $\sigma(c, a) \approx 1$ for all candidates $a$, effectively reducing the score function to a source-weighted count. The constraint ablation experiments in \S\ref{sec:experiments} confirm this degeneration empirically: MAX-SAT, count-only (selecting the answer with the most constraints regardless of satisfiability), and random-weight baselines all produce nearly identical accuracy.
58	
59	\subsection{The C0 Diagnostic}
60	\label{sec:c0}
61	
62	We define the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as the fraction of questions for which the $K$ sampled traces produce more than one distinct answer:
63	\begin{equation}
64	\label{eq:c0}
65	C_0 = \frac{|\{q \in \mathcal{Q} : |\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\}|}{|\mathcal{Q}|}.
66	\end{equation}
67	
68	$C_0$ serves as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering symbolic aggregation. When $C_0$ is low (e.g., below 5\%), nearly all questions are answered unanimously across traces, and SICA produces the same output as SC by construction. Low $C_0$ therefore represents a \emph{necessary condition} for SICA to differ from majority voting. High $C_0$ does not guarantee improvement, as it merely ensures that disagreement exists without ensuring that constraints can resolve it correctly. $C_0$ requires only the trace sampling stage and can be computed before any constraint extraction or solver invocation, making it a useful early stopping criterion.
69	
70	\subsection{Scope and Limitations}
71	\label{sec:scope}
72	
73	The SICA pipeline as evaluated here uses \emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM that generates reasoning traces also extracts FOL constraints from those traces. This design choice isolates the question of whether an LLM's own reasoning can be usefully formalized for answer aggregation, but it also limits the scope of our findings.
74	
75	Our evaluation does not cover trained constraint extractors, external verification oracles, or multi-model cross-extraction setups. Published methods such as DyLoS \cite{zhang2026dylos}, Aristotle \cite{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \cite{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent \cite{luo2025logicagent} achieve positive results on overlapping benchmarks through fundamentally different mechanisms (trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, logic-complete decomposition). Their findings are complementary to, not contradicted by, the negative results reported here. To disentangle extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness, we introduce an oracle variant that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3 (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
76	
```

</details>

Because $c_m$ is derived from the reasoning steps that led to $a_i$, the semantic content of $c_m$ typically entails $a_i$. As a result, $\\sigma(c_m, a_i) \\approx 1$ holds for the source answer, while $\\sigma(c_m, a_j)$ for $a_j \\neq a_i$ depends on whether $c_m$ happens to also be consistent with $a_j$.\n\nThis coupling has a critical consequence. When constraints lack discriminative power (i.e., $\\sigma(c, a)$ is approximately constant across candidates, or constraints from different traces supporting different answers cancel out), the score function degenerates:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nwhich reduces to a weighted count of traces supporting $a$. If constraint weights are roughly uniform, this is equivalent to majority voting. In practice, because self-extracted constraints predominantly encode domain facts rather than answer-discriminative predicates, $\\sigma(c, a) \\approx 1$ for all candidates $a$, effectively reducing the score function to a source-weighted count. The constraint ablation experiments in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} confirm this degeneration empirically: MAX-SAT, count-only (selecting the answer with the most constraints regardless of satisfiability), and random-weight baselines all produce nearly identical accuracy.\n\n\\subsection{The C0 Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:c0}\n\nWe define the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as the fraction of questions for which the $K$ sampled traces produce more than one distinct answer:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:c0}\nC_0 = \\frac{|\\{q \\in \\mathcal{Q} : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}|}{|\\mathcal{Q}|}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n$C_0$ serves as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering symbolic aggregation. When $C_0$ is low (e.g., below 5\\%), nearly all questions are answered unanimously across traces, and SICA produces the same output as SC by construction. Low $C_0$ therefore represents a \\emph{necessary condition} for SICA to differ from majority voting. High $C_0$ does not guarantee improvement, as it merely ensures that disagreement exists without ensuring that constraints can resolve it correctly. $C_0$ requires only the trace sampling stage and can be computed before any constraint extraction or solver invocation, making it a useful early stopping criterion.\n\n\\subsection{Scope and Limitations}\n\\label{sec:scope}\n\nThe SICA pipeline as evaluated here uses \\emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM that generates reasoning traces also extracts FOL constraints from those traces. This design choice isolates the question of whether an LLM's own reasoning can be usefully formalized for answer aggregation, but it also limits the scope of our findings.\n\nOur evaluation does not cover trained constraint extractors, external verification oracles, or multi-model cross-extraction setups. Published methods such as DyLoS \\cite{zhang2026dylos}, Aristotle \\cite{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\cite{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent \\cite{luo2025logicagent} achieve positive results on overlapping benchmarks through fundamentally different mechanisms (trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, logic-complete decomposition). Their findings are complementary to, not contradicted by, the negative results reported here. To disentangle extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness, we introduce an oracle variant that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3 (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n",

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method: The SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nThis section describes Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), the pipeline we implement and evaluate as a representative instantiation of self-extracted symbolic consensus. SICA extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from multiple reasoning traces produced by a single LLM, formulates a weighted partial MAX-SAT problem over these constraints, and selects the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset. We compare SICA against Self-Consistency \\cite{wang2023sc}, which selects the majority answer across the same set of traces.\n\n\\subsection{Overview}\n\\label{sec:method-overview}\n\nGiven a question $q$, SICA proceeds in four stages. First, an LLM generates $K$ independent reasoning traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ by sampling at temperature $T$; each trace $t_i$ yields a candidate answer $a_i$. Second, the same LLM is prompted to extract a set of structured FOL constraints $C_i$ from each trace $t_i$. Third, constraints are deduplicated across traces by syntactic equivalence matching, and each unique constraint receives a weight equal to the number of traces that produced it. Fourth, the deduplicated constraint pool is formulated as a weighted partial MAX-SAT instance and solved by Z3 \\cite{demoura2008z3}. Each candidate answer is then scored by the total weight of satisfied constraints that support it, and the highest-scoring answer is selected.\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) uses only the first stage: it selects $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\mathbf{1}[a_i = a]$, the answer produced by the most traces. SICA's additional signal comes from the constraint satisfaction structure. Whether this additional signal provides discriminative power beyond vote counting is the central empirical question of this paper.\n\n\\subsection{Constraint Extraction}\n\\label{sec:constraint-extraction}\n\nFor each reasoning trace $t_i$, the same LLM that generated $t_i$ is prompted to extract a set of structured FOL constraints. While we prompt the model to produce FOL-style formalizations, the extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers, reflecting the capabilities of instruction-scale models. The extraction prompt requests JSON-formatted output containing the constraint type (equation, inequality, or predicate), a human-readable expression, and a Z3-compatible formula string. Each constraint $c$ is associated with its source trace $t_i$ and the answer $a_i$ that trace produced. A representative extraction output is:\n\\begin{small}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n{\"type\": \"predicate\",\n \"nl\": \"Charlie is kind\",\n \"z3\": \"kind_charlie == True\"}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{small}\n\\noindent The full extraction prompt template is provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}.\n\nWe define the \\emph{extraction rate} as the fraction of traces from which at least one parseable constraint is successfully extracted. Traces that yield no valid constraints are excluded from the MAX-SAT stage but still contribute their answer to the candidate pool.\n\nDeduplication proceeds by syntactic equivalence matching across all extracted constraints. For each unique constraint $c_m$, its weight $w_m$ equals the number of distinct traces that produced a syntactically equivalent constraint. This weighting reflects the intuition that constraints appearing in many independent reasoning paths are more likely to encode genuine problem structure.\n\n\\subsection{Weighted Partial MAX-SAT Formulation}\n\\label{sec:maxsat}\n\nLet $\\mathcal{A}(q) = \\{a : a \\in \\{a_1, \\ldots, a_K\\}\\}$ denote the set of distinct candidate answers for question $q$, and let $\\mathcal{C} = \\{c_1, \\ldots, c_M\\}$ denote the deduplicated constraint pool with associated weights $\\{w_1, \\ldots, w_M\\}$.\n\n\\paragraph{MAX-SAT instance.} We formulate a Weighted Partial MAX-SAT (WPMS) problem where each unique constraint $c_m$ serves as a soft clause with weight $w_m$. The objective is to find the largest-weight subset $\\mathcal{C}^* \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}$ such that all constraints in $\\mathcal{C}^*$ are jointly satisfiable:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3's optimization engine solves this instance with a per-problem timeout of 10 seconds.\n\n\\paragraph{Answer scoring.} Each candidate answer $a \\in \\mathcal{A}(q)$ is scored by the total weight of constraints in $\\mathcal{C}^*$ that support it:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ is determined by Z3: $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if constraint $c$ is satisfiable under the assignment implied by answer $a$. Each constraint is evaluated against every candidate answer, so a constraint extracted from one trace can in principle support or exclude answers from other traces. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-answer constraint evaluation.}\nA constraint $c_m$ extracted from trace $t_i$ (which produced answer $a_i$) must in principle be evaluated against \\emph{all} candidate answers, not only $a_i$. In practice, however, self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answer. Because $c_m$ is derived from the reasoning steps that led to $a_i$, the semantic content of $c_m$ typically entails $a_i$. As a result, $\\sigma(c_m, a_i) \\approx 1$ holds for the source answer, while $\\sigma(c_m, a_j)$ for $a_j \\neq a_i$ depends on whether $c_m$ happens to also be consistent with $a_j$.\n\nThis coupling has a critical consequence. When constraints lack discriminative power (i.e., $\\sigma(c, a)$ is approximately constant across candidates, or constraints from different traces supporting different answers cancel out), the score function degenerates:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nwhich reduces to a weighted count of traces supporting $a$. If constraint weights are roughly uniform, this is equivalent to majority voting. In practice, because self-extracted constraints predominantly encode domain facts rather than answer-discriminative predicates, $\\sigma(c, a) \\approx 1$ for all candidates $a$, effectively reducing the score function to a source-weighted count. The constraint ablation experiments in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} confirm this degeneration empirically: MAX-SAT, count-only (selecting the answer with the most constraints regardless of satisfiability), and random-weight baselines all produce nearly identical accuracy.\n\n\\subsection{The C0 Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:c0}\n\nWe define the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as the fraction of questions for which the $K$ sampled traces produce more than one distinct answer:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:c0}\nC_0 = \\frac{|\\{q \\in \\mathcal{Q} : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}|}{|\\mathcal{Q}|}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n$C_0$ serves as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering symbolic aggregation. When $C_0$ is low (e.g., below 5\\%), nearly all questions are answered unanimously across traces, and SICA produces the same output as SC by construction. Low $C_0$ therefore represents a \\emph{necessary condition} for SICA to differ from majority voting. High $C_0$ does not guarantee improvement, as it merely ensures that disagreement exists without ensuring that constraints can resolve it correctly. $C_0$ requires only the trace sampling stage and can be computed before any constraint extraction or solver invocation, making it a useful early stopping criterion.\n\n\\subsection{Scope and Limitations}\n\\label{sec:scope}\n\nThe SICA pipeline as evaluated here uses \\emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM that generates reasoning traces also extracts FOL constraints from those traces. This design choice isolates the question of whether an LLM's own reasoning can be usefully formalized for answer aggregation, but it also limits the scope of our findings.\n\nOur evaluation does not cover trained constraint extractors, external verification oracles, or multi-model cross-extraction setups. Published methods such as DyLoS \\cite{zhang2026dylos}, Aristotle \\cite{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\cite{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent \\cite{luo2025logicagent} achieve positive results on overlapping benchmarks through fundamentally different mechanisms (trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, logic-complete decomposition). Their findings are complementary to, not contradicted by, the negative results reported here. To disentangle extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness, we introduce an oracle variant that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3 (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n",
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — symb_invariant_consensus\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main (ARR May 25)\\n- Deadline: May 25, 2026 (ARR submission)\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条) — D025 Capability Trap Analysis Paper (FINAL)\\n\\n1. **The Capability Trap**: LLM self-extracted FOL constraint aggregation faces a fundamental tension: it requires output diversity (C0>20%) to differ from voting, but self-extracted constraints lack discriminative power even when diversity is high. High C0 produces abundant constraints, but these constraints fail to distinguish correct from incorrect answers, reducing MAX-SAT to counting. Validated across 3 reasoning domains × 4 instruction-scale model families + 1 full reasoning model (QwQ-32B). [Scope: this finding characterizes same-model self-extraction pipelines; methods using external verifiers or trained extractors may not share this limitation.]\\n2. **Operating Regime is Empirically Empty**: Five model families confirm SICA provides no accuracy gain over majority voting: (a) Qwen-14B n=804 Δ=+0.25pp n.s., (b) Mistral-7B n=60 Δ=0.0pp exact, (c) LLaMA-8B StrategyQA n=200 Δ=+2.0pp p=0.424, (d) LLaMA-8B FOLIO n=204 Δ=-2.4pp, (e) DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B n=60 Δ=-3.3pp. Full reasoning model QwQ-32B shows near-zero diversity (C0<5%), making SICA degenerate to SC. Instruction-scale models (including distilled reasoning models) show high diversity (C0=38-62%) but constraints lack discriminative power. No tested condition achieves both requirements simultaneously.\\n3. **C0 as Necessary-but-Insufficient Diagnostic**: C0 reliably predicts when symbolic consensus CANNOT help (C0<5% → method degenerates to SC), but high C0 does NOT guarantee gains. C0 is a zero-cost early stopping criterion for practitioners considering symbolic aggregation methods.\\n\\n## Core Insight\\nLLM self-extracted FOL constraints don't improve majority voting because of a **capability trap**: the method needs output diversity (disagreement to resolve) AND discriminative constraints (to resolve it correctly). While instruction-scale models produce abundant diversity (C0=38-62%) and constraints, these self-extracted constraints lack the discriminative power to distinguish correct from incorrect answers (precision ~0.50, no better than chance). MAX-SAT optimization over such non-discriminative constraints degenerates to counting, performing no better than simple majority voting.\\n\\n**Critical oracle result (exp-021)**: When gold FOL annotations replace self-extracted constraints, Oracle-SICA achieves +8.82pp over SC (p<0.001, n=204). This proves the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism IS effective given discriminative constraints — the bottleneck is exclusively in LLM self-extraction quality, not in the aggregation paradigm itself.\\n\\nWe provide the first systematic empirical study (a) precisely localizing the failure mode to extraction quality via oracle control, (b) quantifying constraint non-discrimination at per-problem granularity (n=307), and (c) proving MAX-SAT adds nothing beyond counting when constraints lack discriminative power. [Positioning: Logic-LM and LINC documented that LLM-to-FOL translation is error-prone; our contribution is the oracle-controlled causal separation of extraction vs aggregation failure, plus systematic quantification and C0 as deployment diagnostic.]\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n- **Problem**: Recent work proposes augmenting LLM reasoning with symbolic methods, extracting formal constraints from reasoning traces and using solvers for answer selection. But when should practitioners invest in such methods vs. simple majority voting?\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**: SC (Wang et al. 2023), USC (Chen et al. 2023), Logic-LM (Pan et al. 2023), LINC (Olausson et al. 2023), ORM/PRM (Cobbe et al. 2021, Lightman et al. 2023), Weighted SC (Taubenfeld et al. 2024). None systematically characterize when symbolic aggregation helps vs. when it degenerates to voting.\\n- **Our framing**: We instantiate symbolic consensus as SICA (MAX-SAT over extracted FOL constraints) and systematically evaluate across 3 domains × 4 instruction-scale model families + 1 full reasoning model. We find SICA ≈ SC everywhere, and identify a structural reason: the **capability trap**.\\n- **Headline result**: \\\"Across 3 reasoning domains and 5 models spanning instruction, distilled-reasoning, and full-reasoning architectures, SICA provides no significant accuracy gain over SC. Five models (combined N>1,300) show Δ ranging from -3.3pp to +2.0pp, none significant. The constraint ablation reveals MAX-SAT ≈ count-only ≈ random-weight. We identify the structural cause: a capability trap where extracted constraints lack discriminative power regardless of output diversity level.\\\"\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns & Defensive Experiments\\n1. **\\\"Gains not statistically significant\\\"** → Four powered tests: exp-001 (n=804), exp-019 (n=200), exp-020 (n=204), exp-010 (n=60). Combined N>1,200.\\n2. **\\\"Only a better tiebreaker, not worth the overhead\\\"** → exp-002: K ablation (SICA≤SC all K including odd K=11); exp-013: MaxSAT≈count-only (mechanism vacuous); exp-009: overhead analysis\\n3. **\\\"Only Qwen models\\\"** → 4 instruction-scale families: Qwen-14B + Mistral-7B + LLaMA-8B + DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B\\n4. **\\\"Only one reasoning model\\\"** → QwQ-32B (full reasoning, C0<5%). DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B reclassified as instruction-scale (C0=58%, behaves like instruction model due to 7B capacity insufficient for deterministic reasoning chains)\\n5. **\\\"Only logic domain\\\"** → FOLIO (formal logic) + StrategyQA (commonsense) + ARC-Challenge (science). ProofWriter in appendix.\\n6. **\\\"MAX-SAT is over-engineering\\\"** → exp-013: MaxSAT ≈ count-only ≈ random-weight confirmed. This IS the capability trap evidence.\\n7. **\\\"Random tiebreak baseline\\\"** → exp-003: SICA tiebreak 80% vs random 50% vs alphabetical 60%. On tied questions SICA is better, but ties are too rare to matter at scale.\\n8. **\\\"Why not just use a reasoning model?\\\"** → Full reasoning model QwQ-32B shows C0<5%, SICA redundant. Distilled reasoning model DeepSeek-R1-7B behaves like instruction model (C0=58%). Either way SICA provides no benefit.\\n9. **\\\"How does this compare to Logic-LM / LINC?\\\"** → exp-015: Logic-LM × K baseline (CPU-only)\\n10. **\\\"Weighted SC is simpler\\\"** → exp-016: Weighted SC baseline (CPU-only)\\n11. **\\\"C0 is trivially derived\\\"** → C0 is trivial to compute but non-trivial to interpret. Its value is as a zero-cost early stopping criterion, not as a metric per se.\\n12. **\\\"Within-family scaling?\\\"** → exp-004 (7B) + exp-007 (72B-AWQ) if time permits. C0 trend within Qwen family.\\n13. **\\\"Self-extraction confound — same model generates and extracts\\\"** → exp-021 Oracle FOLIO: **RESOLVED.** Gold FOL + Z3 + MAX-SAT = 88.24% vs SC = 79.41% (+8.82pp, p=6.15e-05). Aggregation mechanism IS effective with discriminative constraints. Bottleneck conclusively localized to LLM self-extraction quality. This is now a core contribution, not just a defensive experiment.\\n14. **\\\"Published methods achieve gains on same benchmarks (DyLoS, Aristotle, FoVer)\\\"** → Acknowledge in Related Work and Discussion. Key distinction: those methods use trained/external verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, or logic-complete decomposition — all fundamentally different from same-model self-extraction. Our finding characterizes the specific failure mode of self-extraction pipelines, not symbolic reasoning broadly.\\n15. **\\\"MAX-SAT formulation is just weighted voting\\\"** → Paper must explicitly describe cross-answer evaluation: constraints from trace t_i ARE/ARE NOT evaluated against other candidates. If structurally a weighted vote, acknowledge and frame contribution as \\\"even the most charitable interpretation (weighted vote with constraint-based weights) provides no gain.\\\"\\n\\n## Working Title\\n**\\\"The Capability Trap: Why Self-Extracted Constraints Cannot Improve LLM Majority Voting\\\"**\\n(Alternatives: \\n- \\\"Diversity Without Discrimination: A Systematic Study of LLM-Extracted Constraint Aggregation\\\"\\n- \\\"When Does Symbolic Aggregation Help? Characterizing the Failure Mode of Self-Extracted FOL Constraints\\\")\\n\\nNOTE (Dream D037): Title MUST be scoped to \\\"self-extracted constraints\\\" / \\\"SICA\\\" — NOT \\\"symbolic consensus\\\" broadly. Published methods (DyLoS, Aristotle, FoVer, LogicAgent) achieve gains on same benchmarks using different symbolic approaches. Our evidence covers one pipeline (same-model FOL extraction + MAX-SAT), not the entire paradigm.\\n\\n## Paper Structure (EMNLP 8pp + appendix)\\n- Main Tables: T1 (Main Results: models × domains, SICA vs SC), T2 (Oracle Control: Gold-FOL+MAX-SAT vs SC vs SICA-self, the key causal separation), T3 (Constraint Ablation: MaxSAT vs count-only vs random), T4 (Temperature Ablation: C0 varies 30-43% but Δ=0 throughout)\\n- Main Figures: F1 (Method Pipeline), F2 (Per-problem scatter: vote entropy vs constraint exclusion rate, colored by flip type, n=307 multi-candidate problems from exp-001 — hero figure showing constraints fail to discriminate), F3 (Operating Regime Heatmap: all cells ≈ 0), F4 (C0 vs Gap: experiment-level conditions, marker size ∝ sqrt(n), showing no correlation between diversity and SICA effectiveness)\\n- Appendix: K ablation (exp-002), Per-domain detailed results, Overhead analysis (exp-009), Case studies (exp-008), QwQ-32B/DeepSeek-R1 negative control data\\n\\n## Claim Risk Notes (Updated D035 + exp-014)\\n- **Claim 1 mechanism refinement**: \\\"Inverse correlation between diversity and quality\\\" framing NOT supported by data (experiment-level r=0.15, p=0.74). Correct framing: \\\"constraints lack discriminative power regardless of diversity level.\\\" Evidence: MaxSAT≈count-only (exp-013), high C0 + negative delta (exp-014 C0=58% Δ=-3.3pp, exp-020 C0=62% Δ=-2.4pp). OPEN — need per-problem F2 data from exp-001 to strengthen.\\n- **Claim 2 expanded**: Five models now (added DeepSeek-R1-Distill-7B Δ=-3.3pp). DeepSeek reclassified as instruction-scale (C0=58%, not <5% as predicted). Only QwQ-32B is full reasoning model. RESOLVED.\\n- **Claim 3 gradient → independence framing**: Temperature ablation confirms C0 varies (30%→38%→43%) but gap stays flat at 0.00pp (zero discordant across all temperatures). Data supports \\\"diversity is necessary but insufficient\\\" NOT \\\"gradient diagnostic.\\\" Claim 3 should be: \\\"C0 predicts when SICA cannot help (C0<5% → degenerate), but increasing diversity beyond the threshold provides no benefit — constraint quality remains the binding bottleneck regardless of diversity level.\\\" RESOLVED (temperature data in hand).\\n- **exp-011 +10pp is noise**: n=30 SE≈9pp. exp-020 same task n=204 shows -2.4pp. Do NOT cite exp-011 as evidence of any positive signal.\\n- **F2 hero figure data gap**: Experiment-level correlations (n=7) too weak. Must use per-problem granularity from exp-001 (n≈300+ multi-candidate problems). Agent tasked with this analysis. OPEN.\\n\\n## Required Citations (Dream D037 — minimum 30 references)\\n\\nPaper Agent MUST include these categories (non-exhaustive list of must-cite works):\\n- **SC & extensions**: Wang et al. 2023 (SC), Chen et al. 2023 (USC), Loo et al. 2025 (SC scaling plateaus), Taubenfeld et al. 2025 ACL Findings (confidence-weighted SC), DiVeRSe (Li et al. 2023)\\n- **Neuro-symbolic reasoning (positive results on same benchmarks)**: DyLoS (EACL 2026), Aristotle (ACL 2025), FoVer (TACL 2025), LogicAgent (2025), Logic-LM (Pan et al. 2023), LINC (Olausson et al. 2023)\\n- **Test-time compute scaling**: Snell et al. 2025 (ICLR Oral), DeepSeek-R1 technical report (Nature 2025), Diversity-Aware Training for TTS (2025)\\n- **Verification & reranking**: ORM (Cobbe et al. 2021), PRM (Lightman et al. 2023), Math-Shepherd (Wang et al. 2024)\\n- **Constraint satisfaction in NLP**: SATBench (EMNLP 2025), LLM Meets Constraint Propagation (IJCAI 2025)\\n- **Negative results in NLP**: appropriate precedents for analysis/negative-result papers\\n- **Debate & multi-agent**: Liang et al. 2023, Du et al. 2023\\n- **Reasoning model paradigm**: QwQ technical blog, o1 (OpenAI 2024)\\n\\n## Claim Scoping Rules (Dream D037)\\n\\n- n=60 co\\n\\n... High C0 produces abundant constraints, but these constraints fail to distinguish correct from incorrect answers, reducing MAX-SAT to counting. Validated across 3 reasoning domains × 4 instruction-scale model families + 1 full reasoning model (QwQ-32B). Instruction-scale models (including distilled reasoning models) show high diversity (C0=38-62%) but constraints lack discriminative power. No tested condition achieves both requirements simultaneously.\\n3. **C0 as Necessary-but-Insufficient Diagnostic**: C0 reliably predicts when symbolic consensus CANNOT help (C0<5% → method degenerates to SC), but high C0 does NOT guarantee gains. C0 is a zero-cost early stopping criterion for practitioners considering symbolic aggregation methods.\\n\\n## Core Insight\\nLLM self-extracted FOL constraints don't improve majority voting because of a **capability trap**: the method needs output diversity (disagreement to resolve) AND discriminative constraints (to resolve it correctly). While instruction-scale models produce abundant diversity (C0=38-62%) and constraints, these self-extracted constraints lack the discriminative power to distinguish correct from incorrect answers (precision ~0.50, no better than chance). Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. _source line 741 · 2026-05-17T18:10:01.782Z_
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QMeCBkkQTqf8F2FmGjR1fr`</summary>

```text
1	---
2	name: paper-writing-norms
3	description: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]
5	scope: global
6	version: 2
7	priority: 9
8	tags:
9	  - writing
10	  - paper
11	  - style
12	  - norms
13	roles:
14	  - agent
15	  - worker
16	  - paper_agent
17	---
18	
19	# 论文写作风格规范
20	
21	> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。
22	
23	---
24	
25	## 一、总体写作哲学
26	
27	### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌
28	
29	- 论文必须以"故事线"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证
30	- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进
31	- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌
32	- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing
33	
34	### 1.2 主线统一性
35	
36	- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**
37	- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C
38	- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机
39	- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线
40	
41	### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义
42	
43	- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释
44	- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）
45	- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用
46	- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug
47	
48	### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文
49	
50	- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可
51	- 重点学：
52	  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事
53	  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献
54	  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）
55	  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度
56	- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大
57	- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文
58	
59	---
60	
61	## 二、Abstract 写作规范
62	
63	### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）
64	
65	Abstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：
66	1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点
67	2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）
68	3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）
69	4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献
70	
71	### 2.2 禁忌
72	
73	- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim
```

```json
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/agent-ml-research/skills/global/paper-writing-norms.skill.md",
      "content": "---\nname: paper-writing-norms\ndescription: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。\nallowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]\nscope: global\nversion: 2\npriority: 9\ntags:\n  - writing\n  - paper\n  - style\n  - norms\nroles:\n  - agent\n  - worker\n  - paper_agent\n---\n\n# 论文写作风格规范\n\n> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。\n\n---\n\n## 一、总体写作哲学\n\n### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌\n\n- 论文必须以\"故事线\"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证\n- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进\n- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌\n- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing\n\n### 1.2 主线统一性\n\n- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**\n- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C\n- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机\n- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线\n\n### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义\n\n- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释\n- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）\n- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用\n- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug\n\n### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文\n\n- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可\n- 重点学：\n  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事\n  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献\n  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）\n  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度\n- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大\n- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文\n\n---\n\n## 二、Abstract 写作规范\n\n### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）\n\nAbstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：\n1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点\n2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）\n3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）\n4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献\n\n### 2.2 禁忌\n\n- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim\n- **禁止过度宣传**：不说 \"revolutionary\"、\"groundbreaking\"\n- **禁止写成 Introduction 的缩写**：Abstract 是独立阅读单元\n- **禁止术语堆积**：如果审稿人几十秒内判断不出价值，就失败了\n- **缩写必须定义**：Abstract 中首次出现的缩写必须展开\n\n### 2.3 审稿人视角检验\n",
      "numLines": 80,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 252
    }
```

```json
  "task_prompt": "## Task: Write the Related Work section (~900 words)\n\n### Context\nPaper: \"The Capability Trap: Why Self-Extracted Constraints Cannot Improve LLM Majority Voting\" (EMNLP 2026)\n\nCore finding: SICA (MAX-SAT over self-extracted FOL constraints from K reasoning traces) provides no accuracy gain over majority voting across 5 models × 4 domains (N>1,300). Oracle experiment (+8.82pp with gold FOL) proves aggregation works but self-extraction is the bottleneck.\n\n### Structure (6 clusters, ~150 words each)\nWrite `\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related-work}` with the following subsections using `\\paragraph{}`:\n\n1. **Self-Consistency and Extensions** — Wang et al. 2023 (SC baseline), Chen et al. 2024 (USC), Taubenfeld et al. 2025 (confidence-weighted SC), Li et al. 2023 (DiVeRSe), Loo et al. 2025 (SC scaling plateaus). Position SICA as attempting to go beyond voting with symbolic structure.\n\n2. **Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning** — Logic-LM (Pan et al. 2023), LINC (Olausson et al. 2023), DyLoS (Xu et al. 2026), Aristotle (Li et al. 2025), FoVer (Wang et al. 2025), LogicAgent (Luo et al. 2025). KEY DISTINCTION: these methods use trained verifiers, external provers, or multi-paradigm solvers. Our negative result characterizes SAME-MODEL self-extraction, not symbolic reasoning broadly. State this clearly.\n\n3. **Test-Time Compute and Reasoning Models** — Snell et al. 2025 (ICLR Oral), DeepSeek-R1 (2025), Diversity-Aware TTS (2025). Connect to our C0 diagnostic: reasoning models show low diversity (C0<5%), making any aggregation beyond SC redundant.\n\n4. **Verification and Reranking** — Cobbe et al. 2021 (ORM), Lightman et al. 2024 (PRM), Wang et al. 2024 (Math-Shepherd). These use trained verifiers; contrast with SICA's self-extraction (no trained component).\n\n5. **Constraint Satisfaction in NLP** — SATBench (2025), LLM+CP (IJCAI 2025). Contextualizes our MAX-SAT formulation within broader constraint-based NLP.\n\n6. **Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation** — Liang et al. 2023, Du et al. 2024. Alternative multi-path aggregation. Contrast: debate uses NL refinement, SICA uses formal constraints.\n\n### Citation Keys (from references.bib)\nwang2023sc, chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc, li2023diverse, loo2025reevaluating, pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, luo2025logicagent, snell2025scaling, deepseek2025r1, diverseaware2025, cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2023prm, wang2024mathshepherd, satbench2025, llmmeets_cp2025, liang2023encouraging, du2023improving, huang2024selfcorrect\n\n### Writing Rules (MUST follow)\nRead `/home/ubuntu/agent-ml-research/skills/global/paper-writing-norms.skill.md` before writing.\n\nKey rules:\n- NOT a survey. Goal: position our work relative to existing approaches.\n- Each cluster: summarize the approach category → explain the key difference from our work → state what gap remains that we address\n- NO em-dashes (—) or explanatory en-dashes (–). Use commas or parentheses instead.\n- NO -ing phrase endings (\"..., demonstrating that...\")\n- NO \"Furthermore\" / \"Moreover\" / \"Additionally\" chains\n- NO \"delve into\" / \"leverage\" / \"utilize\" / \"facilitate\"\n- Concise academic prose, not bullet points\n- Each paragraph must have logical flow connecting to the next\n- Final paragraph/sentence: summarize the positioning gap that this paper fills\n\n### Output\nWrite the complete LaTeX to: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/related_work.tex`\n\nUse `\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related-work}` at the top. Use `\\paragraph{Cluster Title.}` for each subsection (saves space vs `\\subsection`).\n\n### Verification\nAfter writing, grep the file for \"—\" and \" – \" to ensure zero em-dashes/en-dashes. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RsA2gteKscNVpoKdzrpz3F`</summary>
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Self-Consistency and Extensions.}
5	Self-Consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer from multiple sampled chain-of-thought traces \citep{wang2023sc} and remains a strong baseline for multi-path reasoning. Universal Self-Consistency (USC) extends SC to free-form generation by having the LLM itself select the most consistent response \citep{chen2024usc}. Confidence-weighted variants assign trace-level confidence scores to improve vote quality \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, while DiVeRSe combines diverse prompting strategies with step-aware verification \citep{li2023diverse}. Recent work has identified scaling plateaus: increasing the number of sampled traces yields diminishing returns beyond a saturation point \citep{loo2025reevaluating}. These findings motivate the search for aggregation mechanisms that extract richer signal from reasoning traces than simple vote counting. SICA represents one such attempt: it replaces plurality voting with symbolic constraint satisfaction over self-extracted FOL formulas. Our negative results show that the bottleneck lies not in the aggregation mechanism but in the quality of self-extracted constraints, a failure mode that vote-based methods sidestep by not requiring formalization at all.
6	
7	\paragraph{Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning.}
8	A growing line of work combines LLMs with symbolic solvers to improve logical reasoning. Logic-LM translates natural language problems into symbolic formulations and delegates solving to an external prover \citep{pan2023logiclm}. LINC pairs language models with first-order logic provers in a modular architecture \citep{olausson2023linc}. More recent systems push this integration further: DyLoS adaptively selects among multiple solver paradigms \citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle decomposes problems through a logic-complete search framework \citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer trains a dedicated verifier on FOL annotations \citep{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent applies multi-perspective semiotic grounding \citep{luo2025logicagent}. A critical distinction separates these methods from SICA. All of the above rely on trained verifiers, external provers, or multi-paradigm solver selection, components that introduce knowledge beyond the reasoning model itself. SICA instead asks whether the same model that produced the reasoning traces can also extract useful formal constraints from them. Our oracle experiment confirms that sound FOL constraints do improve aggregation (+8.82\,pp); the failure is specific to same-model self-extraction, not to symbolic reasoning as an approach.
9	
10	\paragraph{Test-Time Compute and Reasoning Models.}
11	Scaling test-time computation has emerged as an alternative to parameter scaling. \citet{snell2025scaling} show that optimally allocated test-time compute can outperform larger models and establish a framework for analyzing the compute-accuracy tradeoff at inference. Reasoning-specialized models such as DeepSeek-R1 \citep{deepseek2025r1} internalize extended chain-of-thought through reinforcement learning and produce longer but more coherent traces. Diversity-aware training methods explicitly optimize for trace diversity during test-time scaling \citep{diverseaware2025}. These developments interact with our findings through the $C_0$ diagnostic. Reasoning models produce highly consistent outputs across samples: $C_0$ drops below 5\% for DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ on our benchmarks. When nearly all traces converge to the same answer, any aggregation method (symbolic or otherwise) reduces to the unanimous output. The $C_0$ metric thus provides a zero-cost signal for practitioners to determine whether post-hoc aggregation can offer any benefit for a given model.
12	
13	\paragraph{Verification and Reranking.}
14	Trained verifiers offer an alternative path to improving multi-path reasoning. Outcome-level reward models (ORMs) score complete solutions and select the highest-ranked candidate \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}. Process reward models (PRMs) assign step-level scores and enable finer-grained selection \citep{lightman2023prm}. Math-Shepherd automates process supervision annotation and removes the need for human labels \citep{wang2024mathshepherd}. These approaches share SICA's goal of selecting better answers from a candidate pool but differ in a fundamental respect: they use a separately trained scoring model. SICA's constraint extraction requires no training data and no additional model; it relies entirely on the reasoning model's own ability to formalize its logic. Our results suggest that this self-reliance is precisely the problem. Without an external signal to ground constraint quality, self-extracted constraints encode the same biases present in the original traces and fail to provide corrective information.
15	
16	\paragraph{Constraint Satisfaction in NLP.}
17	Constraint-based formulations have gained traction for evaluating and improving LLM reasoning. SATBench benchmarks LLMs on Boolean satisfiability problems and reveals systematic weaknesses in logical structure handling \citep{satbench2025}. Work on integrating LLMs with constraint propagation solvers explores hybrid architectures where the LLM generates candidate assignments and a CP solver enforces hard constraints \citep{llmmeets_cp2025}. SICA's MAX-SAT formulation sits within this broader trend: it uses Z3 to find the maximum-weight satisfiable subset of self-extracted constraints. The key difference is that SICA's constraints originate from the model's own reasoning traces rather than from problem specifications or external knowledge bases. Our finding that these self-extracted constraints lack discriminative power complements the SATBench results: LLMs struggle both to solve formal constraints directly and to produce useful constraints from their own reasoning.
18	
19	\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}
20	Multi-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, a finding that parallels our capability trap: the same model that produced flawed reasoning cannot reliably formalize the correction. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.
21	
```

```json
{
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus",
  "section": "related_work",
  "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Self-Consistency and Extensions.}\nSelf-Consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer from multiple sampled chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc} and remains a strong baseline for multi-path reasoning. Universal Self-Consistency (USC) extends SC to free-form generation by having the LLM itself select the most consistent response \\citep{chen2024usc}. Confidence-weighted variants assign trace-level confidence scores to improve vote quality \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, while DiVeRSe combines diverse prompting strategies with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}. Recent work has identified scaling plateaus: increasing the number of sampled traces yields diminishing returns beyond a saturation point \\citep{loo2025reevaluating}. These findings motivate the search for aggregation mechanisms that extract richer signal from reasoning traces than simple vote counting. SICA represents one such attempt: it replaces plurality voting with symbolic constraint satisfaction over self-extracted FOL formulas. Our negative results show that the bottleneck lies not in the aggregation mechanism but in the quality of self-extracted constraints, a failure mode that vote-based methods sidestep by not requiring formalization at all.\n\n\\paragraph{Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning.}\nA growing line of work combines LLMs with symbolic solvers to improve logical reasoning. Logic-LM translates natural language problems into symbolic formulations and delegates solving to an external prover \\citep{pan2023logiclm}. LINC pairs language models with first-order logic provers in a modular architecture \\citep{olausson2023linc}. More recent systems push this integration further: DyLoS adaptively selects among multiple solver paradigms \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle decomposes problems through a logic-complete search framework \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer trains a dedicated verifier on FOL annotations \\citep{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent applies multi-perspective semiotic grounding \\citep{luo2025logicagent}. A critical distinction separates these methods from SICA. All of the above rely on trained verifiers, external provers, or multi-paradigm solver selection, components that introduce knowledge beyond the reasoning model itself. SICA instead asks whether the same model that produced the reasoning traces can also extract useful formal constraints from them. Our oracle experiment confirms that sound FOL constraints do improve aggregation (+8.82\\,pp); the failure is specific to same-model self-extraction, not to symbolic reasoning as an approach.\n\n\\paragraph{Test-Time Compute and Reasoning Models.}\nScaling test-time computation has emerged as an alternative to parameter scaling. \\citet{snell2025scaling} show that optimally allocated test-time compute can outperform larger models and establish a framework for analyzing the compute-accuracy tradeoff at inference. Reasoning-specialized models such as DeepSeek-R1 \\citep{deepseek2025r1} internalize extended chain-of-thought through reinforcement learning and produce longer but more coherent traces. Diversity-aware training methods explicitly optimize for trace diversity during test-time scaling \\citep{diverseaware2025}. These developments interact with our findings through the $C_0$ diagnostic. Reasoning models produce highly consistent outputs across samples: $C_0$ drops below 5\\% for DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ on our benchmarks. When nearly all traces converge to the same answer, any aggregation method (symbolic or otherwise) reduces to the unanimous output. The $C_0$ metric thus provides a zero-cost signal for practitioners to determine whether post-hoc aggregation can offer any benefit for a given model.\n\n\\paragraph{Verification and Reranking.}\nTrained verifiers offer an alternative path to improving multi-path reasoning. Outcome-level reward models (ORMs) score complete solutions and select the highest-ranked candidate \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}. Process reward models (PRMs) assign step-level scores and enable finer-grained selection \\citep{lightman2023prm}. Math-Shepherd automates process supervision annotation and removes the need for human labels \\citep{wang2024mathshepherd}. These approaches share SICA's goal of selecting better answers from a candidate pool but differ in a fundamental respect: they use a separately trained scoring model. SICA's constraint extraction requires no training data and no additional model; it relies entirely on the reasoning model's own ability to formalize its logic. Our results suggest that this self-reliance is precisely the problem. Without an external signal to ground constraint quality, self-extracted constraints encode the same biases present in the original traces and fail to provide corrective information.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Satisfaction in NLP.}\nConstraint-based formulations have gained traction for evaluating and improving LLM reasoning. SATBench benchmarks LLMs on Boolean satisfiability problems and reveals systematic weaknesses in logical structure handling \\citep{satbench2025}. Work on integrating LLMs with constraint propagation solvers explores hybrid architectures where the LLM generates candidate assignments and a CP solver enforces hard constraints \\citep{llmmeets_cp2025}. SICA's MAX-SAT formulation sits within this broader trend: it uses Z3 to find the maximum-weight satisfiable subset of self-extracted constraints. The key difference is that SICA's constraints originate from the model's own reasoning traces rather than from problem specifications or external knowledge bases. Our finding that these self-extracted constraints lack discriminative power complements the SATBench results: LLMs struggle both to solve formal constraints directly and to produce useful constraints from their own reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}\nMulti-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \\citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, a finding that parallels our capability trap: the same model that produced flawed reasoning cannot reliably formalize the correction. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.",
```

````json
  "task_type": "paper_writing:discussion",
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus",
  "task_prompt": "## Task: Write the Discussion section (~600 words)\n\n### Context\nPaper: \"The Capability Trap: Why Self-Extracted Constraints Cannot Improve LLM Majority Voting\" (EMNLP 2026)\n\nKey results already established in experiments.tex:\n- SICA = SC across 9 self-extraction conditions, 5 models, N>1,300 (Table 1)\n- Oracle-SICA: +8.82pp over SC with gold FOL + Z3 (p<0.001) (Table 1 bottom)\n- Constraint ablation: MAX-SAT ≈ count-only ≈ random-weight, 802/804 agreement (Table 3)\n- Temperature ablation: C0 increases 30%→43.3% but Δ=0 at all temperatures\n- Two failure regimes: (1) constraint-present but non-discriminative (FOLIO/PW), (2) constraint-absent/extraction-degenerate (GSM8K)\n\n### Structure (5 parts, write as continuous prose with paragraph breaks, NO \\subsection or \\paragraph commands for this section)\n\n1. **Oracle as causal intervention** (~120 words): The oracle experiment is not just an upper bound; it is a causal intervention that isolates the extraction-vs-aggregation distinction. By replacing only the constraint source while keeping all other pipeline components identical, we establish that the aggregation mechanism (MAX-SAT) IS effective given discriminative constraints. This design follows precedent from Press et al. 2023 (compositionality gap oracle), Trivedi et al. 2023 (IRCoT oracle retrieval), and Saparov & He 2023 (gold FOL proofs).\n\n2. **Why published methods succeed** (~120 words): DyLoS, Aristotle, FoVer, and LogicAgent achieve positive results on overlapping benchmarks. The key: they bypass same-model self-extraction. DyLoS uses multi-paradigm solver selection; Aristotle applies logic-complete decomposition; FoVer trains a dedicated FOL verifier; LogicAgent employs multi-perspective grounding. Their success is complementary to our negative result, not contradictory. Our oracle confirms that the gap they bridge (+8.82pp recoverable) is real.\n\n3. **Constructive direction** (~120 words): Three paths can unlock the +8.82pp potential: (a) trained FOL extractors (supervised on gold FOL datasets like FOLIO), (b) external provers that verify constraints independently of the reasoning model, (c) cross-model extraction where a different model extracts constraints from another model's traces. The oracle result quantifies the ceiling for each approach.\n\n4. **C0 as deployment diagnostic** (~100 words): C0 is a zero-cost, pre-extraction metric. If C0 < 5%, SICA degenerates to SC by construction. Practitioners should compute C0 from the trace sampling stage before investing in constraint extraction. High C0 is necessary but not sufficient (the capability trap). C0 thus serves as a cheap go/no-go test.\n\n5. **Limitations** (~140 words): (a) Oracle experiment limited to FOLIO (only dataset with gold FOL annotations, n=204); generalization to other domains requires constructing gold constraint sets. (b) Five of nine conditions have n≤60, insufficient for confirmatory statistical power. (c) We do not compare against trained extractors; our finding characterizes same-model self-extraction only. (d) All models are 7B-32B; behavior at larger scales (70B+) is unknown. (e) GSM8K pilot (n=30) included for regime characterization, not confirmatory evidence.\n\n### Citation Keys\npress2023compositionality, trivedi2023ircot, saparov2023greedy, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, luo2025logicagent\n\n### Writing Rules (MUST follow)\nRead `/home/ubuntu/agent-ml-research/skills/global/paper-writing-norms.skill.md` before writing.\n- NO em-dashes (—) or explanatory en-dashes (–)\n- NO -ing phrase endings\n- NO \"Furthermore\"/\"Moreover\"/\"Additionally\" chains\n- NO \"delve into\"/\"leverage\"/\"utilize\"\n- Write as continuous \\section{Discussion} with natural paragraph breaks\n- Include \\section*{Limitations} as EMNLP requirement (separate from main Discussion)\n\n### Output\nWrite to: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/discussion.tex`\n\nThe file should contain BOTH:\n1. `\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}` (main discussion ~460 words)\n2. Leave a comment `% Limitations section is in main.tex` (since main.tex already has a separate \\section*{Limitations})\n\nActually wait - looking at main.tex, \\section*{Limitations} is already a separate block in main.tex (line 46). So discussion.tex should ONLY contain the Discussion section. Put the Limitations content in a separate note that I'll use for main.tex.\n\n### Final output structure in discussion.tex:\n```\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n[Discussion paragraphs 1-4, ~460 words]\n```\n\nThen write limitations content to: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/limitations_content.tex`\n(Just the paragraph text, no \\section command, since main.tex wraps it in \\section*{Limitations})\n\n### Verification\nAfter writing, grep both files for \"—\" and \" – \". Fix any found.",
````
